ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u4	Thank you very much for your words, Madam Vice President. Representatives of the government with the Minister-in-Chief, the honourable fellow Member and Members! We are discussing a very important law because the organisation of navigation services is extremely important for operating or air safety. While aviation accidents are rare, the consequences of such accidents are largely large, if not catastrophic. In order to operate navigation services, it is certainly necessary to ensure that these services operate safely, regularly, smoothly, and also that they are flexible, both in the organisation and in financial and human resources matters. This is certainly the public interest of the State in this area. This law provides for the establishment of a public company that will be 100% owned by the State. This public undertaking is then transferred to all the workers currently employed in this area. It's about 137 workers at the moment. This public undertaking is also transferred to all assets and, at the very beginning of the operation of this company, funds from the budget amounting to 240 million Slovenian tolars are invested. The three services now operating under the Civil Aviation Administration of the Republic of Slovenia are air information services, then air transport services and air telecommunications services, and will therefore, following the new adoption of the law, operate within the framework of this public undertaking, while two other services, namely the air meteorology service and the search and rescue service, remain organised within the Ministry of Environment and Space. In New Slovenia, we have already said, and we still think that it would be more appropriate to organise this service in its entirety, for the reasons I mentioned earlier, there is real security, flexibility, organisational and financial and human resources issues within a single company. Although the government feels otherwise, the majority in this Parliament and after all in New Slovenia will not object to such a solution. However, we are confident that such an organisation will ensure what is expected of it. This is in particular security and, of course, that this public undertaking will perform a service for all airports, a navigation service for all airports, not just for two airports, which has been mentioned several times in the discussion itself in the second reading. We anticipate and hope that the contract, which will have to be concluded under the adopted law between the government and this public undertaking, will be made in such a way as to take this into account. And another thing, let me say, we expect what is being paid to remain at this level, as promised by the government, is that the fee will remain at the level of this 70 euro, which was also discussed in this national assembly. Thank you very much. We will support the law in our parliamentary group.

The fact is that economic services or societies are governed by the principle that their primary purpose is not to create profits, but to provide certain activities that enable the State to make it easier to stay, safety and the like. Our executive branch of power and this Parliament has long since decided to separate between the concept of an economic society and a public economic society. Because companies are set up to make profits. Economic public services are created to ensure public interest. That's an essential difference. However, the Slovenian government, the proposer, is also trying to establish both laws or businesses under both laws. Probably all of you who are also involved in local policy are familiar with cases where local communities, on the one hand, establish businesses on the basis of the Law on Economic Public Services, on the other hand, the same company on the basis of the Law on Economic Companies. And there are such irregularities. The proposer must decide whether or not to have an undertaking responsible for the performance of the tasks covered by this Act and these tasks are important and there is no debate on this at all. Because the fact is, we have to settle this area. ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u21	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen of Parliament!The government is aware that corruption is a serious and dangerous phenomenon and that Slovenia is not immune before this phenomenon. In view of the situation, which appears on the basis of detected cases of various forms of corruption, we are aware that these open cases are not all the phenomena that occur in this area. We estimate that this is not a situation that would require some exceptional non-systemic action. However, it is necessary to simplify and supplement all the regulatory arrangements relating to this area, which therefore seek to prevent the causes of corruption related to the restriction or prevention of conflicts of interest and, of course, to regulate in a uniform and transparent manner all the procedures involved. This is why we are proposing a law to prevent conflicts of interest and to limit corruption. At the same time, you have also received starting points for a resolution on the national anti-corruption programme. The Government intends to submit a proposal for this resolution before the end of its consideration of the Act, thus establishing a legal, regulatory basis for action in this area, while also drawing up concrete measures that are necessary to achieve this objective. So, the basic starting point is for all issues to be settled in a uniform way. For this reason, the law also provides for a circle of persons, officials to whom this law applies. It shall establish an independent institution, a commission to carry out key supervisory functions in this regard. It shall determine the manner in which this Commission operates and, of course, shall contain all the provisions relating to restrictions. Restrictions on gainful activities, restrictions on the acceptance of gifts and restrictions on the business of all persons to whom the law applies. At the same time, the proposed law also uniformly regulates the control of the property status of all persons concerned by the law. It also introduces certain procedures to which State authorities or bodies covered by the law are bound in order to establish effective mechanisms for the emergence of causes, the creation of conditions in which corruption could occur. This is addressed to provisions referring to integrity plans. Therefore, for this explanation of some concrete solutions to this law, further explanation, I would ask you to listen to the Director of the Anti-Corruption Office, Mr Penk.

Of course, the law does not regulate all areas relating to the fight against corruption, but somehow compliments them, complements them. Indeed, the law deals with tackling the causes of corruption and is intended to do so, and therefore, the title of the law, at least in the part where it speaks of limiting corruption. If we limit corruption to an appropriate level that can be managed, then, of course, there are other laws, there are other institutions that, with their powers different from those of this law, must effectively address problems and, of course, this law does not remove them from such behaviour. It is therefore important that you understand the context and purpose of this law. So the law goes much further in a variety of ways than it has been in the current legal system. If we look only at the supervision of the assets of officials, it includes officials in all branches of government and subordinates them to the supervision of an independent institution, it introduces an effective system of control, sanctions, at the final stage with the end of the mandate. This is, of course, decided by the institution within the competent branch of authority, but it also leads to such an end, if necessary, if the official does not respect the procedures provided for in the law. It also covers local officials, which is of exceptional importance, and also includes directors of public services, members of supervisory bodies of public services, institutions, etc., all that has proved necessary in this preliminary discussion somewhere in this issue. Let me also draw attention to the integrity plans, which are an important part of this law and are essentially a process of self-verifying institutions, both in the public and in the private sector, seeking corruption-sensitive areas and then introducing measures that somehow eliminate and effectively prevent corruption in institutions. So the results of this law will not be visible tomorrow, but they will be visible, let us hope that they will be visible in the medium term, and especially in the long term. So much for now. ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u27	Thank you so much for saying that. Dear Minister, the Honourable Director of the Anti-Corruption Office, the Honourable Assembly! The proposal for a law on the prevention of conflicts of interest and the limitation of corruption has only been prepared by the government on a difficult path. In doing so, the fact that he mentions corruption only in the second part of the title as if it were in some way embarrassing for her to exist with us or that she is not ambitious enough and sovereign enough to prevent it and not just restrict it. Although the law has been in existence for two years and there has been a different exchange of views, in our parliamentary group we still believe that this area is so important that a broader debate is needed, more open and not in as narrow circles as it has been. The latest events on the Ljubljana Stock Exchange - more than 500 virtual deals - also show that there are many hidden and hidden events that can reach this area as well. In this regard, we would be interested and would ask the Director of the Anti-Corruption Office whether he could answer how these events affected or how these events could affect the statistics and thus the classification of our country in the international space; perhaps there is much to be said about this. What is corruption? There is no comprehensive and complete definition of corruption in our legal order. There are individual corruption offences in criminal law, as well as in the bond law we find cases that are bound to obsolete periods. Whatever our opinion is on the work of the Anti-Corruption Office, we can say that what has failed to the officials of the Ministry for many years has succeeded the Office in clearly defining what corruption is. Corruption includes unlawful acts and practices that are unethical, immoral, constitute an abuse of the law and constitute an abuse of an individual with a certain position for private purposes. The integration of policies and economies, as well as, for example, education, health care, especially in the stages where regulations are adopted in certain areas, when a future legal order is established, when capital is redistributed from the state to the private sphere, when various para-State institutions and organisations are formed that will have significant consequences for their actors in the future. Following the adoption of a certain law, a sub-law or regulation, a situation may also arise where corrupt cooperation between policy, economy, education, health or other structures creates a monopoly, a privileged position of individuals or groups and a discriminatory position of others. Thus, a high concentration of power in the hands of the ruling economic and political elite. In doing so, we should certainly not forget those practices that are corrupt in nature and in nature, but people as such do not perceive or even accept them, tolerate them, land on them, and even execute them themselves without bad conscience. These are a number of actions in the context of small, small-scale corruption that occurs in contact with various public services and in the private sector and without which the average citizen is almost no longer able to represent everyday life. The law of such conduct does not usually incriminate in the sense of prohibited acts, offences or criminal acts, because their individual dangers against the law and value are insignificant. Many times, even such acts are necessary in everyday contact, in human relations, as they are based on different mutual, friendly, related, and other relationships. However, joint action and its cumulative effect are so large and no less important than systemic corruption. They mean the corruption of human relations, relations between individuals and the country and society. So it is a matter of cases where an individual has to pay for what he would be entitled to for free when a person is found in an unequal position, when he should have the same position under the Constitution and the law, and according to moral and ethical standards. Corruption in itself threatens democracy by promoting distrust of the authorities. This denies the principle of social justice and leads to a fall in the welfare of an average citizen when he redirects financial resources into the pockets of privileged individuals and groups. On the other hand, it promotes mistrust in people, creating an atmosphere of general social mistrust and, last but not least, distorts and blocks development incentives in the economy. The fact is that corruption exists and is increasing in Slovenia. Regarding its scope, the estimates vary. The 1998 opinion of the population shows that 58% of the population surveyed agreed that corruption has increased with us after the change in the political system. The first general survey on corruption in our country since 2002 has shown that citizens' assessment of the prevalence of corruption is greater than the actual experience in this area that citizens have. A survey among economists also showed that 43% of Slovenian managers believe that corruption is a major problem. For a long time, the ruling elite closed their eyes to this phenomenon. Although one of the parliamentary groups tried to legislate in 1994 in this area, the government rejected it by arguing that there was no need for corruption in Slovenia, and since then it has grown very large in Slovenia, by already jeopardising the interests of some in power. The government did not wake up until 2000, after the problem was raised in the GRECA report that there was no authority in Slovenia that would deal only with corruption and coordinate anti-corruption activities. In 2001, the government set up a coordination group to prevent corruption as an interdepartmental body within the Ministry of the Interior. A few months later, her tasks were transferred to the Anti-Corruption Office as a self-sustaining professional service. The purpose of the office was not to prosecute corruption, and they were in charge, clearly, of the prosecution and of the police. However, the public expected much from the office and its operation. Indeed, on the one hand, the Office was preparing analyses of the situation in Slovenia, a strategy against corruption, and in the final stage also a law against corruption. However, the management of the Office has taken on a role in which it has not been possible to resist an assessment of some cases of possible fraud and legality and corruption, with a number of allegations of this Office not having received any epilogue and a number of investigations have not yet been initiated. This, in turn, caused strange and not very good feelings among people. A comparative review shows that anti-corruption laws, including independent anti-corruption institutions, exist in all countries. Many countries have an institution with great powers. They act preventively and repressively and have the power to deal with corruption by bringing an indictment, and they also have the appropriate means of investigation and methods to detect it. The government has therefore prepared for us a new bill combining anti-corruption matter with the incompatibilities of performing a public function with a profitable activity with the office. The last one's been inconclusive for years. In fact, since we were adopted ten years ago. Although the Commission of the National Assembly has put forward a number of proposals to improve the legal text, the government has not taken this into account. For a number of years, the decision of the National Assembly, which ordered the Government to prepare legal amendments, was unfulfilled. The current law does not establish a single system within the meaning of the debtors, does not define the official sufficiently well and leaves essential solutions to other laws that do not exist at all. Specially attractive is the area of local officials for whom no supervision has been established, and there is not at all a single mechanism and effective sanctions. The fact is that the existing law does not provide a comprehensive system of control over the assets of officials. I would like to stop a little with some of the solutions proposed. In the first place, I would mention a significant novelty, namely the identification of who are the officials and their tax numbers, namely those defined in the Constitution and also extending to officials in state authorities and local communities, the establishment of an independent and independent body, a commission for the integrity of public action, and the Council of the Commission as the supervisory body of the Commission. In our parliamentary group, we have a certain fear before the establishment of such a commission, we are skeptical, we are afraid that this will be another commission in a number of committees that will not be able to do so with the tasks and powers it has, what it has to do. We believe that much more should be done in the area of persecution: the police, prosecutors and courts should play a key role in this area. Greater professional responsibility within the bodies, greater supervision, less free assessment of procedures and clearer definitions of the delimitation of policy and economic areas would be needed. It would then also stop in the regulation on the incompatibilities of the performance of a public function with a profitable activity, while commending the expansion of the range of officials, which has been lacking in the regulation so far. Corruption should be as criminal as all other delinquents, we believe, and the country should encourage repressive authorities to do more effectively, introduce additional expert groups, increase mutual cooperation, and even increase penalties for certain crimes, if necessary. Also in the area of receiving gifts is a proposed solution for which further improvements and improvements are worth looking for. An explicit prohibition is laid down for receiving gifts, with the exception of those donated in accordance with protocol rules and for occasional gifts of minor value. A value of 15,000 tolars or a total value of 30,000 tolars per year from the same person and also applies to family members of officials. The accepted gifts and their value should be entered in the list of gifts to the authority in which the official performs the function. We ask ourselves whether the sum of 15 thousand is truly symbolic of most Slovenians, whether they can afford such a gift from the Alpine or another factory. We also ask ourselves the following question. An official or an official in the municipality may, on a daily basis, cash out a few times after 15,000, as he issues a large number of different permits, various documents. Perhaps it would be a better solution if the gift was tied to the official once a year worth 15,000. In any case, we understand that it is difficult to avoid just everyday situations, such as lunch invitations or other occasions. Therefore, it would be important to discuss the purpose of the gift, not the amount. That's a few of our comments. Moreover, the question of how seriously does the government deal with this law. It is known that 30 % of the applications received at the office relate to public procurement and therefore very important activities are in preventing corruption in this area. The current Public Procurement Act does not specify that any contract on the choice of an operator or supplier must contain a so-called anti-corruption clause. Such clauses are intended for possible subsequent findings that corruption has occurred in the past and provide for the cancellation of the deal in its entirety or the nullity of the contract. Nor does the proposal for a new law in this area provide for the introduction of a mandatory anti-corruption clause, nor does it specify the consequences for any other country linked to the transaction of a supplier or contractor found to be a bribe, since the undertaking will not be included as a so-called blacklist of companies which are not entitled to tender for corruption. The public opinion poll conducted by the Anti-Corruption Office last year certainly shows that corruption is now more important than before 1990, according to citizens. The population in Slovenia has been increasingly divided and divided for some time now, with members of the upper layers prepared and able to pay more and more for various services, either for education, for health, social, economic. On the other hand, we have that group of people who provide services, and mainly because of materialistic thinking, which actually predominates with us, a pattern of payment of reception services. This situation, we are sure, is unsustainable for the country. One and the same people are always waiting in long lines for different services, while the other, privileged, are subject to different rules. It is clear from the point of view of the legal, just, social and democratic state that such a situation is unsustainable, so we believe that the law before us, and it is meant to actually intervene in this area, cannot do so, and that it is too strong, has too little teeth to actually bite to be effective, so that it could be the tangible core of our society, that it is too much of a diversion, and only for apparent readiness. And as a result, in our parliamentary group, we feel that marriage is not good and we cannot support it. Thank you very much.

The United Nations Convention also recommends that the State ensure greater public competence in decision-making processes; to ensure effective access to public information; to promote greater sensitivity to corruption; to develop education programmes on corruption phenomena, which should also be included in public school programmes and also in university study curricula; to respect and promote the freedom to seek, obtain and publish information on corruption; to ensure anonymous access to public anti-corruption institutions and to ensure that they can effectively communicate all information on corruption phenomena set out in this Convention. ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u41	Thank you for your word. Dear Minister, Director, Colleagues and colleagues! In opening the debate on the draft law, I must stress that in the Parliamentary Group of the Young Slovenia Party we are committed to regulating the area under consideration. However, we also have many comments on the bill, and we also stress the need to take more account of the Commission's long-standing experience and comments under the law on the incompatibilities of performing a public function with a profitable activity when preparing and adopting the law. Article 1 of the draft law defines its content, which implies that the law should cover two areas. On the one hand, the competent commission is expected to continue the work of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia to prevent corruption by carrying out strategic-analytical tasks in the field of corruption, coordination tasks and advisory and educational tasks. However, individual corruption remains a crime. And as such, the subject of criminal proceedings. On the other hand, the Commission is specifically supposed to exercise control over only a certain group of persons where corruption may occur. So, control over the assets of the officials, which has been carried out by the Commission so far under the law on the incompatibilities of performing a public function with a gainful activity. The purpose of the Incompatibilities Institute is to ensure that, through the supervision of the official’s assets and thus by controlling the restriction of the activities of contracting entities under public procurement rules with business entities in which the official or his family member is involved, the acceptance of gifts and the non-compatibilities of individual functions or activities shall ensure the objective performance of the function or remove any doubt about the possible partial exercise of that function. By carrying out tasks under the Act of Incompatibilities of the performance of a public function by virtue of a gainful activity, the Commission shall be subject to the supervision of a commission of some preventive measure against possible corruption on the part of a particular group of persons, officials. Sanctions are not penal in terms of criminal law. Therefore, we do not see any reason why the performance of the tasks in these two areas should be brought together, since, on the basis of the proposal for a law, the competent commission should act on the one hand in an analytical, strategic, coordination and advisory and educational manner, while at the same time specifically exercising control over certain persons, which, by law, is already carried out by the Commission on the incompatibility of the public function with a gainful activity and, of course, by other bodies. As already stated by the proposer, on the grounds of the adoption of the law, GRECA proposed a group of anti-corruption countries at the Council of Europe to set up a coordination body for all authorities, institutions and sectors dealing with the prevention and detection of corruption, making their concrete work easier and more effective, but not for the incompatibility of functions. I would like to point out that in Article 2 of the draft law, the officials are not state lawyers and social defenders, since the latter are already identified by the Commission under the law on the incompatibilities of the performance of a public function by virtue of a gainful activity or control. However, by further laying the groundwork for defining the concept of 'functioner', which is an open question to which this Commission has also been alerting all the time, the decree by which the National Assembly, under the second paragraph of Article 10 of the Public Sector Wage Scheme Act, will place individual functions in pay grade, which is also our proposal to list officials who are obliged to give their assets an overview. The proposed law is also intended to lay down fundamental rules in the areas of non-compatibilities in the performance of a public function with a lucrative activity for officials, restrictions on the acceptance of gifts in respect of the performance of a function, supervision of the assets of officials and restrictions on the business of contracting entities under public procurement rules with business entities in which a member of his family has been involved. Therefore, it is intended to lay down the basic rules already covered by the Act on the Incompatibilities of the performance of a public function performed by a special commission and other bodies, such as the Council of Justice, which, in its work, implements the provisions of that Act. Thus, since 1993, that Commission has been carrying out tasks which, following a proposal for a law on the prevention of conflicts of interest and the restriction of corruption, should be carried out by a State authority, namely the Commission on the Integrity of Public Action, in which the President and the Deputy should be employed full-time, while the current members of the Commission, under the Act on the Incompatibilities of the performance of a public function with a gainful activity, should carry out these tasks within their respective functions, as they are Members and Councillors of State. An additional number of civil servants should also be employed for the smooth work of this new Commission, which in itself is an additional burden on the state budget. Furthermore, the bill provides for a two-tier system. In order to oversee the performance of the Commission's tasks, the Council of the Commission is to be established, whose members would hold office in honour, but would have the right to reimburse the costs incurred in connection with work in the world. The Council is to supervise the work of the Commission by reporting to the President of the Commission and oversee the assets of the President, the Deputy and the members of the Commission. In view of the number of members of the Council, the reimbursement of the costs of work in the world and the fact that the Council would exercise control in essence on the basis of the report of the President of the Commission, but would in fact directly monitor only the property status of the members of the Commission, the question is whether such an authority is really necessary, so that its composition does not also cover all representatives of the individual types of officials. It follows from the draft law that the Commission should also monitor the operations of contracting entities under public procurement rules with business entities in which an official or his family member has a share of more than 20% and, in the event of an infringement, have also punished them for the offence. Does this make sense in view of the fact that other authorities are already responsible for controlling the consumption of public contracts? Nor does the proposal for a law bring significant innovations in the field of restricting the exercise of gainful activities, acceptance of gifts and business. So, news that would fill open issues, which has been pointed out by the Commission all the time by the law on the incompatibilities of public functions with gainful activity. Indeed, the proposal of the law still does not solve the problem of defining the concept of an official, nor does it specifically mention everyone. The draft law does not regulate, in any way, any other than the existing Act on the incompatibility of a public function with a gainful activity and therefore does not solve cases where the official begins or continues to carry out an activity or function that is incompatible with the performance of his or her function. The Commission is to inform the competent authority thereof, and this concludes the competence of the Commission, the competent authority is only obliged to return information and nothing else. Also gifts. The gift may also constitute debt waiver or waiver of rights, which may present a problem for us to determine that the gift has been given in connection with the performance of the function. Article 40, which provides for the discharge of an official who does not provide the required data on property status, is also controversial. Failure to submit data may first form the basis for disciplinary responsibility and fines, but not automatically and solely a solution. What about the worst violations since we do not know Sharia law in Slovenia and we will not cut our hands? How, then, will others be punished if we punish them for such an initial misdemeanour? In view of the Government's existing decision of 13.7.2001 to establish a Anti-Corruption Office and whose tasks it lays down, on the basis of the draft law on the prevention of conflicts of interest and the restriction of corruption, a Commission on the Integrity of Public Action and on the existing Act on the Incompatibilities of the performance of a public function with a profit-making activity, this task is carried out by the Commission under the law on the non-compatibilities of the management of a public function with a profit-making activity, it seems reasonable, in the light of GRECA, that the area of the first Commission should be governed by the relevant law and that the tasks of the second Commission should be amended and the existing law should be supplemented in the direction of its more effective enforcement, in particular by addressing the most frequent issues and problems faced by the Commission under the law on the non-compatibilability of a public function with a gainful activity, in particular since the proposal is already in Parliament's procedure. In the Parliamentary Group of the Youth Party, we believe that legislation in this area needs to be amended or supplemented to ensure the effective enforcement of existing mechanisms, such as the participation of tax authorities. Regarding sanctions, positive legislation should be taken into account in order to lay down penalties that would be enforceable. In particular, a special solution should be found for Members as representatives of the people who may be deprived of individual functions rather than a mandate. In short, the operational application of legal provisions should be ensured within the limits of the given powers. In accordance with your consideration of our comments, we will decide in the Parliamentary Group of the Youth Party whether we will also support the complementary proposal of the law.

It is up to us alone whether and how Slovenia will resist the phenomenon that has already led to the collapse of economic, political and social systems in countries that have dared to commit corruption and prevent it. Of course, there are two ways to deal with problems. By accusing each other of who and why it is to blame that we are not doing any better in this area than we are, I hope that it is even going, namely, by washing dirty laundry from the whole of the self-sustainment period or by making an appropriate and calm assessment of the situation, and by taking appropriate measures to ensure, on the one hand, full respect for fundamental human rights and freedoms and at the same time establish what is necessary for what in the most civilized countries forms the basis for a fair, transparent and responsible implementation of obligations in all situations and in all areas: from the President of the State to the mayor of the least municipality, from the State administration to private enterprises, corruption can appear everywhere and conditions for preventing it must be created everywhere. We must not turn a blind eye to the fact that in our short history we have witnessed illegal, immoral and similar misconduct in almost every possible position and in every possible form. At the same time, we also know that, according to the results of both domestic and foreign surveys among the most honest of comparable countries, we are also better than some prominent members of the European Union. So there is no need for sudden, reckless or revolutionary solutions, nor do I think there is any excuse for our possible complete passivity. Some countries, such as Lithuania, Croatia, are dealing with corruption through the establishment of authorities with maximum police powers almost in a way close to emergency situations. Such solutions represent a grave mistrust of the regular detection and prosecution authorities, extend the repressive state apparatus and its powers to an uninterrupted extent. The possibility of judicial and parliamentary control over the work of these authorities is reduced to formalism at the time of their flood, the rights and freedoms of the individual remain at the back. I estimate that there is no reason in Slovenia to follow these trends. Our criminal legislation, by all criteria, is taking a step with that in the most developed countries of the European Union. The authorities of detection, persecution and trial, despite their desire to do better, still have confidence. Corruption is still not a daily way of life, despite the fact that it is undeniably much more than official data shows. There is no reason, I suppose, to interfere with extreme solutions, to drink and to refine those that have already been established with us. That is exactly what I think is the purpose of the law that we are starting today. With completed solutions in the field of conflict of interest prevention, through the prohibition of the pursuit of profitable activities, with appropriate sanctions for living and internationally comparable arrangements for the reception of gifts in the workplace and with a complete system for monitoring the property situation of state officials at all levels and all three branches of government, we will try to reduce opportunities for corruption to a minimum. Instead of being fragmented and amputated by various bodies, we are establishing a new body, an independent and professional state commission, which will only be in line with the one which such a body in modern society can meet: this is, for us, the members of the national assembly to the representatives of the legislative branch of power. In addition to this, the law brings something that Europe - the United States of America and Australia - does not already know - is the integrity plans that are compulsory in the public sector and recommended in the private sector, after which care will be taken about the ethical activities of employees in the first instance of their employers' tasks, only then the forced state apparatus. Personally, I think that we will be able to make the solutions - as we will design them - in the second reading, properly complemented by all the evidence accompanying the draft law submitted to us by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia at first reading. Therefore, I think that this is a solid journey, so that we can cope with phenomena that we certainly do not want in our society. It therefore grieves me that, in fact, we who now have the law in the procedure do not accompany it with political unity, given the awareness that we must also inform our public that the law is now in such a state that we can complement it, correct it and send it on the road with a sign of the political unity of all of us in this House, representing citizens and citizens, that at least the politicians are prepared to face together in a uniform manner the phenomenon which can be a society such as the Slovenian youth society, pollute to such an extent that it will no longer be pleasing to people in such a country. This is how I personally ask my opposition colleagues, whose discussions I have closely monitored, to support the proposal of the law at first reading as a suitable basis for work in the context of the second reading, and thus to show unity in dealing with these phenomena. I respect all their comments dealing with the effectiveness of the work of the executive branch, but I am of the opinion that their comments concerning this law are not such that they could not be incorporated into the law itself at second reading. So I sincerely hope that with political unity and, of course, with respect to this law, we will lead the way. It is only in this way that we will invite people whom we estimate to help prevent such acts with their awareness to fight corruption together. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u75	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Dear Members and Members! A number of opinions, critical comments, comments on the proposed law were expressed. First of all, it's about the concept of the law itself. Of course, any marriage proposal must be based on some pre-democratic discourse and on some genuine will of most of those who are concerned by the law or who are engaged in it. In this case, we have actually tried everything to find out what Slovenia needs in the fight against corruption and what it is that we can do in these contexts. This discourse, which did not take place and not only ran in the government, but also wider, all those who wanted to participate or could have been activated. Thus, the general public, which has been, I can admit, quite uninterested as well as civil society, which is practically absent in this area, as well as representatives of educational institutions, universities and so on, who first requested a fee for any opinion, not to mention. So all those whom we considered and who could contribute in any way to the improvement of the substance of this law were invited to debate and had the opportunity. In the end, we're actually, I'm going to say, more or less lonely when we went on with this project. But in the widest possible way, we have tried to find out what Slovenia needs in this area. And what we have found is that there is no consensus in the direction that we need some pressure that will bring some institution that is similar to the institutions that have been established by countries that have found that they are all corrupt, that everything is wrong, that the police are ineffective and corrupt, that the prosecution is not doing its job, that the supervisory institutions are not doing their job, that the elite authorities are closed, and that it is impossible to control power, etc. Therefore, these findings did not form and crystallize in this discourse in such a way as to give the applicant the opportunity to draw up such a law, of course, which would then violate the principles of the division of power, violate the principles of the delimitation of powers, etc., and actually form a strike fist that would strike with all means after corruption. It was clearly established, and it was also professionally clear that we have all these competent institutions in Slovenia, and that we have a satisfactory legal basis, and that more or less the problem of the inaction or inefficiency of these institutions is the uncoordinated nature of their work in relation to the human factor of the actors led by these institutions and those working in these institutions. It turns out that when someone who wants to work comes to the top of the institution, there are immediate visible measures. It is also evident in the public that this institution has powers and that this institution can work. It is therefore pointless and dangerous to create a new such institution to fight corruption, to fight corruption crimes. Corruption is a much broader problem than crimes. Of course, we are also fully aware of this. We have estimated that the large deficit, in addition to the inefficiency of institutions and the inefficiency of controls, is also in the field of ethics and integrity. That is why we have intervened in this area with the proposed regulation. I estimate that a large, extremely large step forward has been taken with this, and by this I am giving all recognition to the government that it has allowed the Anti-Corruption Office, which has always been very loud and very critical to the government, this will probably not be denied by anyone to exclude and become an independent institution that can also oversee the government itself. So an institution which will, in principle, respond to the National Assembly, which will be, at least in the sense of the functionaries who lead, also formed, consolidated, and which will have all the powers that the Office now has, by means of an impartial, independent autonomous position that will allow for any necessary criticism, need to intervene in accordance with this new proposal of the law. It is important to know that the law will be followed by a resolution on the national programme or on the origins of the fight against corruption, which will be very detailed and which is inseparably linked to the law itself. This future independent institution, which is irrelevant, will have the task of ensuring the implementation of this resolution. As you have seen in the starting points, which are now annexed to the explanations of the law, this resolution intervenes in all areas - civil society, state institutions, legislative measures, practices, cooperation and so on - so practically everything that must be combined into an anti-corruption initiative. And the important mandate and task of this future institution is to take care of the implementation of this strategy. In the two new important areas that we have already mentioned - now I cannot in detail - I assure you that we have made every effort to build the most consistent and effective system of control over the property situation of all government officials with material provisions that are subject to this control. Of course, there's probably something else that can be fixed with the amendments. I also assure you that the integrity plans, which are also one of the essential parts of the law, are a project that we are already implementing in the form of pilot projects in some institutions, which have therefore expressed readiness and desire. This is a project that can in fact lead to a significant improvement in the situation in institutions in the long term, which does not even know where corruption-exposed cities occur. This is actually about self-examination. As you can see from the marriage, the commission advises on this. Otherwise, the law requires the institutions of the state administration and the authorities of the local government to have such plans, but nevertheless it is essentially a matter of self-initiative action within the institution itself, which can mean a substantial improvement in what the institution gives out. This is exactly the project we were working with the Scandinavians, whom some of you referred to. Integrity is in their home, it's practically their concept, they've actually brought it into this European space. They also helped us to understand and formulate this part of the law. So it's actually a set of measures, a set of ways and methods to work to address the causes of corruption. However, we have never claimed that this law would interfere with the better treatment of crimes in order to achieve greater efficiency of the police, the prosecution and so on; there are, of course, other rules and other measures that must ensure that the work will also be better. And in that I fully agree with you. I would also like to ensure that we who work at the office do not, in fact, have any pretensions. Even if we do, they are legitimate in terms of working in this future institution. The procedure is open, everyone will be able to run for office as a member of this commission, president, deputy. The National Assembly will be the one to confirm this candidacy or not to confirm it. Applicants are also different. All that concerns us is that - because our body is being abolished and because the new body represents continuity to the old body - we become public servants with this future envisaged institution. This is all the privilege we have, which I don't think is any privilege, because otherwise we would be working elsewhere in the government if this institution were to be abolished. It is also worth saying about rationalisation at this point. The government is currently abolishing five offices by law. For this office, however, we have in some way established, through the debate, that it should not be abolished, but that it should be made an independent institution, just for the reasons that I have listed. For the first time, this independent institution will take over all the employees of the current office. It is true that it will need some new workers, but it will replace all the many administrations that should, in other words, be set up and set up to control the assets of officials across individual branches of government, [[...]] [[A warning sign for the end of the debate.]] by individual institutions at the level of local self-government and so on. ARTICLE OF THE NATIONAL GOVERNMENT LAW RELATING TO OTHER AND THIRD PARAGRAPH 103. Member of the Law of Administration. The proposal for the adoption of an authentic interpretation of the first paragraph of Article 83 of the Law on State Administration relating to the second and third paragraphs of Article 103 of the Law on Administration was submitted by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration by the assembly. I believe that the Secretary of State, Dr Virant, will want to explain the proposal further. He's got five minutes to do that.
ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u83	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen of the House! From 1.1.1995 he established administrative units and transferred the powers of municipal administrative authorities to administrative units. The logic of the delegation of power was also followed by the logic of the transfer of the right to use the premises and equipment by which these tasks were carried out. The Administration Act provides in Article 103 that administrative units shall carry out work at premises and through funds used previously by municipal administrative authorities for the areas of those administrative tasks carried over from 1.1. This has created some kind of public law restriction on the property rights of the municipality with the right of use by the State. In particular, administrative units against the payment of a proportional part of maintenance and operation costs. The new State Administration Act, which was adopted last June, also raised the issue of ownership and use of administrative units. At that time, we also considered the choice of ownership or transfer of property, co-ownership of an ideal share in the country, which would certainly be a legal solution. However, in the discussions, in particular in coordination with local community associations, this solution would have proved unacceptable for the municipalities. In such a way that the State Administration Act did not include this solution. The Law on State Administration on this issue is silent. Because Article 103 of 1.1.1995 has been consumed. That's when it came to pre-social property ownership. Therefore, the establishment of the municipality's ownership right, while at the same time restricting the right of use in favour of the administrative unit against the payment of a proportional part of the operating and maintenance costs. Following the adoption of the Law on State Administration, there were some explanations that the State no longer has a basis for the use of these premises. Some municipalities have tried - I can say - to blackmail by offering the conclusion of a lease, i.e. classic civil-law leases. We have therefore decided to draw up an authentic interpretation that would dispel doubts that would make it clear that the repeal of Article 103 of the Law on Administration does not also mean that the relationship created by this provision of Article 103 will change or end. Any other interpretation would be incorrect and also harmful because it would jeopardise the exercise of national functions at local level. Once again, there was also an option - and the country would have the right to do so - an option of ownership, transfer of property or at least an ideal share in premises from municipalities to the country. That's it! The Law Service has given its position, which is, however, rejected until the authentic interpretation. But not because I would disagree with that explanation. But on the contrary, in her view, this explanation is completely clear and therefore unnecessary. Rather, the provisions are clear and do not need further interpretation. In view of the knowledge of practice, however, we feel that due to legal certainty and to the avoidance of unnecessary disputes and excuses, it is still better to accept this authentic explanation. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u84	Thank you very much. The proposal for an interpretation was addressed by the Committee on Internal Policy as the parent working body. I would like a member of Mr Miran's committee to run for the Committee's report.
ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u85	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Dear colleagues and colleagues! The Internal Policy Committee shall propose to the Sorghum Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia that the proposal for the adoption of an authentic interpretation of Article 83(1) of the Law on State Administration in relation to Article 103, paragraph 2, and Article 103, paragraph 3, of the Law on Administration shall not be adopted. We note that the proposal for the adoption of an authentic interpretation, which formally contains all the required ingredients in Article 149(2) of the Rules of Procedure, has been made on the grounds that there has been an interpretation by individual municipalities that the new Law on State Administration has entered into force in July 2002 and has repealed the Law on Administration by not providing provisions on the relations between the municipality and the State with regard to the premises and assets contained in Article 103 of the repealed Law on Administration - has intervened in this relationship in such a way that they now need to be re-ordered, but in accordance with the law governing civil-legal rental relations, not by the provisions laid down in the Law on Administration. The Secretary of State also spoke about this. The mother’s working body addressed these issues. It followed the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service that the new Law on State Administration did not affect the issue of material assets, including premises, nor did it destroy the application of this regulation in the repealed Law on Administration. The new Law on State Administration, by repealing the previously applicable Law on Administration, has not abolished or amended the regulation, since it should, in other words, explicitly contain it because it concerns property matters. Therefore, the premises and assets concerned are subject to such property relations, including the legal rights which impose this property, as established under Article 103 of the Administration Act. This provision has regulated ownership. In order to further address this issue, it is not necessary to re-examine this issue, nor should this fact, which follows from the law itself, be reconfirmed by a mandatory interpretation. The opinion of the parent working body is that the law governing the material relations between the new owners and the administrative authorities is valid. That contracts concluded on this basis also apply, that there is no need to re-regulation and that the adoption of a compulsory interpretation in such a case would be contrary to the principles applicable otherwise to the content of the compulsory interpretation. It is therefore not a vague provision that should be reexplanated. It seems to us that all these issues should be highlighted clearly and unequivocally in the home working body. In order to remove any doubts as to the possibility of requiring a new regulation of ownership relations. The ownership relations are regulated and must run under contracts concluded under the repealed Article 103 of the Law on Administration. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u97	Yeah. Well, this topic of local offices, I don't think it's directly related to the authentic explanation. But I'm going to give you an answer so that it doesn't go unnoticed. It is true that the criteria and content of the local offices are within the competence of the Ministry of the Interior and that this issue will be regulated by a regulation, but with a quite different content as you know it. In fact, we've been preparing this rule for quite some time. Because there is a dilemma to be resolved. The rational organisation of administrative units should be considered or, on the one hand, balanced and, on the other hand, the need to bring services closer to users. Or if I simplify, we want people to be as close to service as possible and, on the other hand, we cannot agree to this in a given budgetary situation, so that people in local offices sit without work. Well, the final solution to be adopted in the rules is very flexible. For the first time, it no longer defines any fixed kilometre conditions. But you don't have the latest version of the rulebook. Another time. It shall leave a decision to the Chief of Management on condition that it has the staff and financial conditions provided. Third time. We are not monitoring, we will not monitor the effectiveness of individual local offices, because we are not interested in this as a ministry. But we remain in the system of monitoring the efficiency of the administrative unit as a whole. So we're not going to measure the work of that referee who works at the local office, but we're going to look at the productivity of the administrative unit as a whole through the new system of administrative statistics. Fourth time. The system is set up to provide a uniform range of services that citizens, citizens and legal persons will receive in local offices, unlike before. We got the whole catalog. In addition to those services listed there - this is about 40 of the most basic administrative services - the Regulation also states that the local office provides all information about all other administrative services, it is said that a point of information is served. Flexibility is also in the fact that the Chief of Management will decide how he will handle the official hours. Thus, the local office will only be exceptionally - this is possible two cases in Slovenia - will have the status of an organisational unit that will operate 5 days a week, 8 hours a day. That'll be an exception. Otherwise, it will be flexible according to the needs of the local population. Twice a week, three times a week, once a week. Maybe even during some administrative actions, such as passport swaps and so on. So, maximum flexibility. But that's all I can say about space. Often the question was - and this question is, say, connected with the use of premises by the State, and this was often open - is it right for municipalities to provide their space for the functioning of the administrative unit? Is it right for municipalities to lend their computer to the administrative unit? We are limited by the resources intended for the operation of administrative units. The municipalities are prepared to come across and space and not transfer the computer to its own, but to transfer it back to the use of the country, using the administrative unit. The benefit is mutual: the country gives the person, the person, the person, once, twice, three times a week if necessary, and the congregation comes to her with its premises, office, and computer on the container. We estimate that this system is good and that we will keep it for the future. We have over 90 computerized local offices. We'll only keep those who will be included in the information spine. Local offices in the old sense of home offices are unnecessary. They make sense where they provide a comprehensive service to the customer, the user.

ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u99	PROPOSAL FOR TRANSITIONAL EXPLANATORY REASONS 218. Member of the Buildings Act. The proposal for the adoption of an authentic interpretation of Article 218 of the Buildings Act was submitted to the National Council of the Republic of Slovenia for consideration by the assembly. I give the representative of the Council of State the floor to further explain the proposal. Mr. Fras, please.
ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u100	Thank you very much, Mr President, for your word. Well-welcome fellow Members, fellow Members. As a representative of the Council of State, I will try to explain our request or proposal for an authentic explanation of Article 218 of the Buildings Act. Although I must say in the introduction - as you may have understood from the material or those who are dealing a little more with local self-government or also with the municipalities, the way in which the compensation is given, you probably already know - that a number of these reasons, which were the main reasons for this initiative, have already been eliminated since the Ministry of the Environment and Space for postponing this requirement has already issued a supplementary instruction that eliminates one of the essential misunderstandings. In our view, there are still some outstanding issues. So allow me to complement or further support our initiative. The main reason for this initiative was, in fact, an explanation, or "compendium", as appointed by the Ministry of Environment and Space, which was published or I think is still on the Ministry of Environment and Space website and where local communities are to be interpreted twice after a new compensation for walled buildings. As a sum, for the first time, the refund applied to the useful commercial or residential area of the building located on that land. And once again from the surface of the land on which this facility stands. Such a practice did not have a basis in the law or is not recorded in Article 218. Perhaps the legislator was going to write something down here, but it wasn't written like that. This was the main and basic reason for us to request or give an initiative for the authentic interpretation of the law itself. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia has defined our request and, as its opinion also suggests, does not oppose the requirement of an authentic explanation. The government has proposed an amendment to this requirement for an authentic explanation. This is because our request for an authentic explanation explains or eliminates only a misunderstanding or ambiguity regarding the way in which the compensation is given for this building site. It does not solve or answer the question of how the differences between the building and the building site will now be identified, or what data will be used to make those differences. It is said that between the area of the whole plot and the area for which only the compensation for the building plot is to be given and the area remaining on that plot, for which compensation should be measured as for the building plot that is not built. I believe that this issue or the interpretation of this matter may seem somewhat complicated. I'll try to make it as simple as possible. The point is that nothing changes in unburied buildings. We get two situations with built-up building sites. One situation is when a building plot is established on this building site as such. That's when the replacement is measured the way it was. So it's from the useful or business area of the facility. However, as long as such building site is not specified - it is said - the locational land has not been issued or the subsequent functional land for this facility has not been determined, then the refund for such land shall be administered twice. Once for building land, for that part of this land, which represents the foundation of the building, increased by factor 1.5. For that part of the area of this land, the refund shall be the same as before, it shall be said, of a useful or residential area. Now the difference between the area of this foundation of the object increased by 1. 5 and the whole area of the plot, and for that difference of surface, measure the compensation for the building land. You can probably imagine what a project this is for local communities. This means that it is necessary to individually check each plot, to determine whether it has a specific - I am talking about building land, so that there is no misunderstanding - a building plot. If he's got it, fine. If it does not exist, find the foundation of the object that stands on this plot, multiply it by 1. 5 and this area shall be deducted from the entire surface of the building. In short, it is a very large scale, a very difficult task, which will also take time in the local community and, finally, money for this matter. I stress this in particular from this context, because the applicant or the Ministry of the Environment and Space only notes that the remuneration is granted only in the preceding period until this instrument or this public financial benefit or the tax is reimbursed as such.

On the basis of these, it is possible to determine the areas from which compensation is paid, both for the walled area and for the walled building area. ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u105	Thank you very much. It is now up to the members of the groups. Mr. Mamić.
ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u106	I welcome all present. I thank you for your word. Therefore, the position of Nova Slovenije in relation to the authentic interpretation of Article 218 of the Buildings Act. This interpretation raises the question of how to give compensation for the use of buildings according to the new definition of building land following the last year's Law on the Construction of Buildings. Following the regulation so far, this compensation for unpaved building land is paid from the whole area of the unpaved building land. On the other hand, for the building’s walls, it pays from the residential or business area of the building, which is located on the building’s plot. In the case of multi-story buildings, this means that the compensation for the use of the building land shall be awarded for each floor, not only for the floor or for the land on which the building is situated. We're talking about land. According to Article 218 of the Construction Act of last year, construction plots with construction buildings and construction engineering buildings are considered to be walled buildings. With such a definition, the municipalities were afraid that they would from now on be able to charge compensation for the use of the building land even in skyscrapers only from the floor floor or the area of the building plot on which this building stands, not from the floor of all floors. Such an interpretation would be detrimental to the municipalities whose income is compensated for the use of building plots. On the other hand, I would relieve the owners of buildings liable to pay this tax. The municipalities consider that the law should not thus reduce one of the most important sources of revenue from the municipal budget, with which a wide range of funds for economic and social development are also provided. Or, in other words, money for the mayor's and other municipal officials' salaries. Therefore, the Council of State requested an official interpretation of that provision of the law by allowing the municipality to continue to collect compensation for the use of the building land of each floor of the multi-story buildings, not only from the area of the plot on which the building lies. To comfort the congregations. In the New Slovenia Group, we first ask ourselves this problem: Why are we still counting on building owners to contribute to the use of building land under the 1984 Act, which was a socialist law, which considered this contribution to be some kind of rent for building land that was then only socially owned? At that time, property relations have only changed so that the owners of buildings can now be real owners of private land, not just users of state land. This contribution should therefore at least be renamed a contribution to municipal equipment or the like. In no way can he continue to give the impression that he does not own all property rights on his land or in the building. If this interpretation is judged from a tax point of view, we believe that the regulation so far is wrong. However, the owners of several floor buildings for the same building land must pay several times more than the owners of the floor buildings. In this way, luxury and irrational land use is promoted. On the other hand, they are penalized by high-level owners who live above each other in blocks or towers. All of these interpretations, however, are aware that in this country the financing of municipalities is very poorly regulated, which is why they must also help themselves with such revenues when compensation is paid for the use of building land. We therefore agree with the formal explanation submitted that, irrespective of the text of Article 218 of the Buildings Act, municipalities may continue to account for the contribution to the use of building land in the current manner. However, such a position does not oblige the Government to be liable to provide the municipalities with as soon as possible with other more well-founded sources of income and to abolish the contribution to the use of building land as a form of taxation. Consideration would also be given to the taxation of habitable land which owners do not wish to sell. In addition to the fact that the municipality would have a richer budget, I believe it would also be a contribution to the improvement of the demographic problem, which is far from being achieved in Slovenia. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2003-10-02-SDZ3-Redna-29.ana.u131	Dear President, Mr Minister, co-workers, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! In the Liberal Democracy Club of Slovenia, we will be giving a decree on amendments and amendments to the decree on the transformation of the University of Maribor, which, by establishing a medical university and including the Faculty of Police and Security Sciences, complemented the internal structure of the University of Maribor, in accordance with its development programme. The content of the decree follows the definitions of the National Higher Education Programme in the Republic of Slovenia, where we wrote that, in meeting the conditions for quality work, it is possible to properly redesign the current study programmes and introduce new ones in line with the development plans of universities, the staffing situation in particular areas and the employment needs of the country and regions. Special attention will also be paid to health care, with the possibility to study in particular medicine, information technologies and language studies being expanded as soon as possible. Although efforts have been made to establish another medical faculty in Slovenia for a number of years, they have been particularly intensive in the last two three years, with the participation of the National Assembly very actively. A thorough analysis of the situation and comparison with a number of European countries showed a relatively large lag with the supply of doctors in Slovenia, and the need for doctors was further demonstrated by the unpleasant age structure of doctors. On the basis of the positive decision of the Higher Education Council of the Republic of Slovenia on the compliance with the law of certain conditions for the establishment of the University of Maribor Medical Faculty, a clear assessment of the Minister of Health is made that by 2020 Slovenia will need 1,100 doctors or about 25% more than now, and the conclusion that the University of Ljubljana Medical Faculty for the specific and nature of the study itself cannot meet these needs by increasing the entry. In support of the amendment of the decree, it should be pointed out that preparations for the establishment of a medical university at the University of Maribor, in cooperation with the Maribor General Hospital, were carried out at all times. The experience of the Maribor General Hospital, which has already been fully involved, as well as some other hospitals in Slovenia, has borne an important burden on the extension of admissions to the Faculty of Medicine in Ljubljana, as learning skills and skills from clinical subjects requires work at the hospital. In particular, it should be pointed out that the drafters of the study programme have always led to the requirement of international comparability, quality and modernity, which is essential for entry into the common European space. This includes the experience of various European countries in the study programme, the experience of teaching at the Faculty of Medicine in Ljubljana and the coordination and cooperation between the two Faculty of Medicine in Slovenia will allow for the free passage of students between the two faculties. The establishment of a medical faculty in Maribor and the implementation of an internationally comparable study programme will have a number of other positive effects in our opinion. This will increase accessibility for these studies, as the number of admissions to medical studies has virtually not increased in recent years, with an extraordinary increase in the number of students. Thus, a number of candidates who have continued their medical education in neighbouring countries or who have opted for other study programmes for objective reasons will have the opportunity to achieve their objectives. It is also of paramount importance that the development of the Faculty of Medicine in Maribor will also ensure a higher level of health services, as the latest achievements of science can be applied more quickly in practice, and it will certainly be possible to detect a positive impact and raise medical knowledge and health services in hospitals, not only in Maribor, but in the whole northeastern region, Slovenia and, we are sure, also in the wider European area. In doing so, particular emphasis should be placed on the support of the establishment of the Faculty of Medicine of the University of Maribor of the type of institutions of northeastern Slovenia, which have already provided, within the consortium, the considerable resources needed to cover the start-up costs. The amendment of the decree represents an important step in development also for a high police-security school, which as a college for police and security sciences will significantly complement the internal structure of the University of Maribor. From the very beginning of 1973, when the Higher Administrative School in Ljubljana set up the Internal Affairs Education Department, the idea of a single school was also growing by supplementing the content of the study programme. By adopting the proposed decision, we want all the necessary activities to be carried out in the future, so that we can turn on the green light to the first generation of enrolment in the school year 2004-2005 and that the implementation of the study programmes in those faculties would make an important contribution to the realization of the knowledge-based society, one of the fundamental development objectives of the Republic of Slovenia. Thank you very much.
